Katalog Berita (Halaman 57/57)

#195. Gold for Palestine: Meet junior karate champ Mariam Bsharat

The occupied West Bank is not an obvious breeding ground for sporting success. But Mariam Bsharat, 17, has beaten the odds to become a karate world champion and is making a new life in Germany.

"After the referee lifted my hand to show I'm now a two-time world champion, it was a very overwhelming feeling," Mariam Bsharat said. "Making history once is something, but making it twice is really something else. I felt very happy and I felt a big relief. Only I know how many sacrifices I made and how many hardships I needed to overcome."

Themes of relief, joy and sacrifice are commonplace in the reflections of athletes after a big win. In Mariam Bsharat's case, it's about more than just dedication to training and taking care of the body. The 17-year-old won her second under-21 World Karate Championship gold medal competing for the Palestine national team in Poland in October.

"It was everyday life, seeing people get killed around me and houses getting bombed," she told DW of her upbringing in the occupied West Bank.

"I faced challenges in transportation," Bsharat said. "I wasn't able to go from one city to another to be able to train with other people, so that I can actually prepare for competitions. I needed to travel to other countries like Jordan or Egypt ... which was very hard for me. And, financially, the support wasn't really the support an elite athlete needed."

Growing up in Nablus, Bsharat had a narrow path to the top. But initially, it was her own reluctance that threatened to stop a champion career before it started.

"When I was a little kid, I did not really love it," said Bsharat, who competes in the division for fighters who weigh under 66 kilograms (145 pounds).

"I started it because my family was in that sport," Bsharat said. "My father had a dojo, and my mother also did that sport. My siblings and my aunts and my uncles — it's a family thing. I started it because they were all doing it. In time, I started getting into it."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Family has been a constant source of comfort and inspiration for Bsharat. Her father and uncle have acted as manager and coach respectively. While her mother and siblings moved with Mariam to Hamburg, Germany, last year, her father remained in order to take care of his business, the dojo, though he visits and tries to be with his daughter in competitions. Integration has been made easier by her mastery of the language, taught to her by her German mother.

Bsharat has mixed feelings about leaving her homeland.

"I'm thankful to be here because being in Germany opened lot of doors for me, and I think I have a brighter future here," Bsharat said. "But also being in Palestine was just my everyday life, and I loved it there. My family was around me, my friends were around me, and I just felt comfortable, which was a big change coming here to Germany. I think every change comes with hardships."

As well as the obvious difficulties of living in an area occupied by Israel, there are particular challenges for sportspeople living in the West Bank and the Gaza Strip, with access to facilities, travel and constant security fears among them. As such, very few people from the Palestinian territories become successful on the international sporting stage. That, Bsharat said, gives her responsibilities that are unusual and heavy for a person of any age, let alone a 17-year-old.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Being an elite athlete in Palestine is something very special because not everybody in Palestine has that opportunity. There aren't really a lot of athletes that are elite and who win international medals,” Bsharat said.

"So all that pressure from all the country and all the people, the family, the coaches are on me because I'm representing them, raising their flag,” she said. "I sometimes even forget to fight for myself and actually think about myself."

That has not yet proved a barrier to Bsharat, who is is ranked first in the world in her category by the World Karate Federation (WKF). With Palestine recognized by the International Olympic Committee, Bsharat would likely have been a contender to raise that flag in Los Angeles at the 2028 Olympics. But the sport was dropped from the Games in 2024.

"The global karate community remains steadfast in our belief that the natural place for karate is within the Olympic Games, and we sincerely hope that the incongruence we suffered in Paris 2024 and LA 2028 will be corrected for Brisbane 2032, if not sooner," the WKF wrote in response to the decision in 2024. The sport was also taken off the slate for the recent Youth Games in Senegal.

Should karate return in Brisbane, Bsharat would likely be approaching her prime. In the meantime, she is looking forward to starting her journey as a senior when she turns 18. But she won't be hanging around.

"My goal is it to be a world champion at seniors, which is the hardest and the best thing you can achieve in karate," she said.

"But winning an Olympic medal, being called Olympic champion or just representing my country with that flag [is a thought that] is just full of happiness and pride."

The interview was conducted by Janek Speight.

#191. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#189. The Pitt and ER are similar enough for Michael Crichton’s widow’s lawsuit to proceed, court rules

Court finds ‘clear and numerous similarities’ between two medical dramas, allowing lawsuit by ER creator’s widow Sherri to go to jury trial in 2027

The Pitt is similar enough to ER to allow Michael Crichton’s widow to pursue a long-running lawsuit over the award-winning medical drama, a US appeals court has ruled.

In a unanimous opinion filed on Wednesday, a three-judge panel denied an appeal from Warner Bros Television (WBTV), which has argued for two years that the shows are unrelated.

“There are clear and numerous similarities between The Pitt and ER,” the court ruled, citing the fact that both shows are set in an emergency room, and feature “frequently incomprehensible” medical jargon, overlapping crises and graphic depictions of injuries. “The doctors and nurses are exhausted; there are emotional outbursts,” the ruling reads. “Each includes a young doctor, fresh from medical school and incompetent.”

The defendants – WBTV, along with John Wells, executive producer of The Pitt and formerly an ER showrunner; his company John Wells Productions; actor Noah Wyle, who starred in both ER and The Pitt; and R Scott Gemmill, who was executive producer and writer on a number of ER episodes and is now the showrunner and an executive producer of The Pitt – had argued these elements were “generic similarities” common to “any dramatic story involving emergency room doctors” and that it “cannot be that all shows set in an emergency room are derivative of ER”.

But on Wednesday the court ruled: “We find the details here suffice for a prima facie case.”

The suit will now go to a jury trial in Los Angeles, unless the parties reach a settlement.

The author and screenwriter Crichton was the creator of ER, which was based on a film script he wrote about his experiences as a medical student. His 1994 contract for ER with WBTV included a “frozen rights” provision, which blocked any spin-offs or reboots of his work being made without his approval. Crichton died in 2008.

His widow, Sherri Crichton, filed her suit in August 2024, five months before the first episode of The Pitt debuted. She alleged that The Pitt was an unauthorised ER reboot, and accused Wells of “personal betrayal”.

The appeals court ruled on Wednesday that WBTV must go to trial, but dismissed Wyle, Wells and Gemmill as defendants, finding no evidence that any of them were aware they could violate Crichton’s contractual rights by creating The Pitt.

Wyle, Wells and Gemmill have all acknowledged in interviews that The Pitt was created because the ER reboot failed to get off the ground, but only because they had already come together to make a medical drama about the experiences of health workers during the pandemic.

“We were going to start down an ER reboot road that didn’t come to pass,” Wyle said on Amy Poehler’s Good Hang podcast, adding that because they’d assembled to plan that show with HBO Max “we were then allowed to really fast-track the development process [of The Pitt] because of that vacuum that we created from the near-miss”.

Wyle described not having to do the ER reboot as freeing, saying: “Once we uncoupled ourselves from that IP, it suddenly liberated the storytelling. It wasn’t about ‘who do we see again’ and ‘what have they been up to’. It’s ‘what are the issues of the day’ and ‘what’s the most cogent way to put them across’ … [it] allowed us to reframe and kind of modernise it, and not have to pay any homage to travelled road.”

WBTV had filed a motion to throw out Sherri Crichton’s lawsuit under California’s anti-Slapp statute, which is intended to stop meritless lawsuits that curtail freedom of speech. But the judge, Wendy Chang, denied that motion in 2025, finding that Sherri Crichton had enough evidence to proceed.

“I am deeply gratified by the Court of Appeal decision and look forward to discovery and to our case, which affects writers, creators and their families and estates, being presented to a jury,” Sherri Crichton told Variety on Wednesday.

The Guardian has contacted WBTV for comment.

The Pitt has won 12 Emmys, two Golden Globes and a Peabody over two seasons, with Wyle winning multiple best actor awards for his performance as Dr Michael “Robby” Robinavitch, a grizzled chief attending doctor working in a Pittsburgh emergency department. On 11 seasons of ER, Wyle played Dr John Carter, a rich young doctor working in an under-resourced emergency department in Chicago.

Season three of The Pitt will start broadcasting in January.

#184. US midterm elections 2026 live: Democratic lead grows in Mississippi Senate race as Republican blame game begins

Scott Colom leads Republican Cindy Hyde-Smith by 1,138 votes with 96% of the vote counted – a small but growing margin

Tell us: how do you feel about the midterm results?

After Democrats secured historic wins in last night’s midterm elections, Republicans are beginning the blame game:

Donald Trump claimed Republicans fared poorly because he was not on the ballot – after repeatedly urging voters to pretend he was on the ballot.

JD Vance pointed to Trump’s ongoing war in Iran as the “obvious” culprit for the loss.

Pete Hegseth’s role leading the unpopular war could lead to him taking the fall – despite Trump publicly backing him. Republicans including Trump foe Thomas Massie have criticized the defense secretary.

Ron DeSantis, Florida’s governor, said the “close to an extinction-level event for Republicans” was caused in part by his party failing to target their ad spending on issues that would motivate voters.

We’re still awaiting results in a few key races:

Currently, Democrat Scott Colom is leading his Republican incumbent Cindy Hyde-Smith by just 1,138 votes with 96% of the vote counted – in what could be a historic upset in Mississippi.

In Alaksa, Democrat Mary Peltola is leading Republican Dan S. Sullivan by just 626 votes. If neither candidate receives 50% of the vote, the Alaska Senate race will move to rank-choice voting.

Meanwhile, in the Los Angeles mayoral race, the battle between the Democratic incumbent, Karen Bass, and her progressive challenger Nithya Raman, a city council member, is also still too close to call. Only 63% of the vote has been counted so far, and Raman has a narrow lead.

This blog is now closing, but our live coverage of the 2026 US midterm elections is continuing here.

As we reported earlier, votes for Scott Colom in Mississippi are starting to outpace those for his Republican opponent in what has been an otherwise neck-and-neck race for the state’s Senate seat.

According to the most recent tabulation from the Associated Press, Colom leads Republican Cindy Hyde-Smith by 1,138 votes with almost 96% of the vote counted – still a small margin, but increasingly significant after a lead of just 28 votes yesterday.

Strong support in majority-Black counties has helped make the seat in Mississippi unexpectedly competitive for Democrats. As of noon Wednesday, Colom had received about 73% of the vote in those counties while running neck-and-neck with Hyde-Smith statewide.

Republican Senator Ashley Moody, who won her reelection in Florida on Tuesday by a closer margin than many polls suggested, has told Fox News her party really needs to “drill down on affordability.”

Moody has joined a chorus of voices from across her party who have admitted that Donald Trump and his party failed to make the case to voters that they were focused on bringing down the cost of living.

double quotation markWe thought we were delivering historic tax cuts and a housing bill … but the people want more, they want us really focused on that. I think you’ll see us get back to Washington and really drill down and be accountable to what folks want.”

Iran this week unveiled a huge billboard and banners over Enqelab Square in central Tehran. The billboard pledged in English: “We will kill Trump” – elevating the degree of personal animus with which Donald Trump is viewed in official Iranian circles.

The poster depicts the US president behind bars with his head bowed. And in a sense, given the US midterm election results on Tuesday, Iran has indeed killed Trump, or at least the second term of his presidency. Trump’s reverse is all the more piquant because it comes on the day Iran’s leadership celebrates the anniversary of the seizure of the US embassy in Tehran in 1979, an event that ensnared Jimmy Carter, and a reminder of how Iran has ruined the prospects of more than one US president.

Not surprisingly, the keenly followed election results have led to a good degree of triumphalism inside Iran.

Ehsan Movaheidian, an international relations scholar, claimed: “The Islamic Republic of Iran has succeeded in regime change in America. The same will soon happen in Europe as well. Bow down before the power of Iran.”

Meisam Zohurian, a conservative member of parliament, said: “The joy over the outcome of the US elections has nothing to do with the arrival of this party or the departure of that party. The issue is the aggressor was smeared in the dirt and Iran had an impact on the result.”

Ali Nikzad, the first deputy speaker of the parliament, said: “These electoral defeats are the aftershocks of Trump’s defeat by the Iranian people.”

Iran’s supreme leader said anyone expecting a shift in US policy was “seriously mistaken”, however. “Those who still harbour even the slightest hope that the behaviour of the hegemonic system might change through partisan or factional shifts, or who imagine that making concessions to the United States can dissuade it from hostility towards the Iranian nation, are seriously mistaken,” Mojtaba Khamenei wrote.

But the big question now is whether either side will regard these seismic results as a moment to review their diplomatic strategy. Iran’s chief negotiator, Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, was quick to call on Trump to accept the wisdom of the American people. On social media he urged Trump to bow to the verdict of America: “Whether driven by conscience or the cost of living, America said no to war and we endorse this message.”

Many congressional members of the House Black Caucus survived the mid-decade redistricting push that was designed to dilute their districts. Experts suggested that the resilience in their vote reflects an unusual turnout surge, but warn the maps behind the push remain in place.

After the supreme court redistricting ruling this year, caucus chair Yvette Clarke warned that up to 19 seats were at risk from Republican-led efforts to redraw districts, and not all of those seats were on the ballot on Tuesday.

Many caucus members held on with convincing wins – including Texas’ Christian Menefee and Missouri’s Emanuel Cleaver – while some had more narrow victories, like Alabama’s Shomari Figures and North Carolina’s Don Davis.

But law experts cautioned against interpreting the midterms as a permanent fix to the altered electoral maps.

“This kind of turnout can’t be a once-in-an-election-cycle kind of phenomenon,” said Melissa Murray, an NYU law professor.

In Tennessee’s 9th district, one of the congressional districts redrawn to favor Republicans earlier this year, the Democratic candidate has conceded defeat.

The Memphis-area congressional district that was one of several southern districts represented by Black Democrats that were redrawn in the wake of the US supreme court decision weakening the Voting Rights Act.

Democrat Justin Pearson said Wednesday that he came close to victory in the gerrymandered district and noted that “Tennessee politics will never be the same.”

double quotation markI accept the results of this election for Congress. But, I will not concede to rigged maps, attacks on democracy in Tennessee, or to billionaire control of our political system.”

As Republican leaders from across the country continue to mull over their historic midterms loss, the White House continues to insist it’s business as usual.

In a post on X, the White House account writes “We aren’t going anywhere”, adding:

double quotation markLock in, patriots. We’ve only just begun.”

In a news release earlier on Wednesday, the White House laid out what it believes is the “promises” made and kept to the American people, as Trump’s allies scrambled to respond to Republicans’ stunning electoral defeat.

double quotation markEvery time the Radical Left, the Fake News, and the professional panic class have written President Trump’s obituary, they have been wrong. They will be wrong again.”

It comes after Trump and many of his allies have acknowledged the Iran war’s effect on his party’s inability to maintain control of Congress, suggesting voters were frustrated by surging gas prices that have been a byproduct of the conflict.

Elon Musk set a record for election spending this year – contributing at least $446m to Republican-aligned Super Pacs and groups - in the US midterm elections. It turned out to be a poor investment.

Democrats won every one of the five Senate races where Musk’s America Pac spent the most money backing Republican candidates. In Texas, where Musk’s empire is based, Republicans lost the Senate race, governor’s office and are expected to relinquish control of the state house.

My colleague Nick Robins-Early has the full story:

Democrat Teresa Benitez-Thompson has won her race for the House of Representatives in Nevada’s 2nd district, flipping a Republican stronghold.

Benitez-Thompson defeated her Republican opponent David Flippo and will fill the seat of retiring congressman Mark Amodei, who’s represented the district for more than a decade.

Zohran Mamdani, New York City’s progressive mayor, said the midterm election results “speak to the crises in working class Americans’ lives” in an interview with CNN.

“I think last night what we saw was a reflection of the fact that working class Americans are tired of being told that their needs are unrealistic by politicians who have been bought by the wealthiest people across the country,” he said.

Democrat’s wins are “a mandate to drive down costs, a mandate to deliver change working-class Americans can feel in their wallet, and a mandate to fulfill the trust that those same Americans have put into Democrats,” Mamdani added.

Progressive candidates, like Mamdani, who is a member of the Democratic Socialists of America, have experienced record wins in the past year. According to a DSA candidate tracker, the organization is poised to see at least 135 candidates win their races this year.

Voters rejected datacenters, the facilities full of semiconductor chips that power artificial intelligence and a host of other online products, in elections across the country last night.

Proposals regarding datacenters were on the ballot in elections in at least 40 jurisdictions across 10 states. Voters approved nearly every datacenter moratorium that was on the ballot, dozens in total, with one notable exception: Hardwood, North Dakota.

My colleague Johana Bhuiyan has the full story:

Democrat Dave Min has won his bid for re-election to the House of Representatives in California’s 47th district. Min defeated his Republican challenger Jenny Rae Le Roux.

Democrat Marni von Wilpert has won her race for the House of Representatives in California’s 48th district, flipping a seat long held by a Republican.

von Wilpert, a member of the San Diego city council, defeated Republican Jim Desmond in a contest to fill Republican congressman Darrell Issa’s seat after the 13-term congressman announced his retirement.

Former Republican House speaker Newt Gingrich said “the scale of the Republican defeat” in the midterms “is so great that it makes the race for the GOP presidential nomination wide open.”

In a social media post, Gingrich wrote that while JD Vance might have previously been the obvious frontrunner for the Republican nomination in 2028, yesterday’s election results open the door to a number of new candidates.

“The number of people who want to draft Secretary of State Marco Rubio is staggering,” he wrote. “In addition, Ted Cruz, Tom Cotton, Ron DeSantis, Pete Hegseth, Glenn Youngkin, and a surprising number of people may decide this is their chance to take advantage of the chaos of the shocking defeat and seek to be the champion who can rescue the party and the country.”

The White House has scheduled a hearing into allegations of mortgage fraud by Federal Reserve governor Lisa Cook for 14 December, the New York Times reports, citing an unnamed White House official. Donald Trump has been working to fire Cook for more than a year, in an effort that would mark the first time a president has ever removed a member of the Fed.

Here’s our latest reporting on the effort to fire Cook:

Texas senator Ted Cruz, a Republican, posted a lengthy analysis on social media of his state’s staggering shift blue in yesterday’s midterm elections, calling the results “an ass kicking” and “Watergate-level repudiation.”

“Our voters, Republicans, Libertarians, Independents, showed up and voted, and rejected our candidates, voting Democrat instead,” Cruz wrote, adding “Nowhere are the consequences of this election greater than Texas.”

Cruz blamed Republicans from failing to address issues of affordability, writing, “The top issue, cost of living, is very real. Too many in politics dismissed it.”

“We will rebuild as a true blue-collar party, not by embracing socialism, but by being real free-market conservatives,” he said."

#181. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#179. Husband who invited men to rape wife asked for an online review, court told

Nine men on trial in Manchester accused of either taking part in abuse or encouraging offending on internet

A husband who invited other men to rape and sexually assault his wife arranged for “more and more individuals” to visit his marital home as he resolved “to satisfy his own perversity”, a jury has been told.

The husband, who is in his 60s and from Stockport in Greater Manchester, has pleaded guilty to more than 60 offences against his wife. He cannot be named in order to protect the anonymity of his victim.

Nine men are on trial at Manchester Minshull Street crown court accused of either taking part in the abuse of the victim, or encouraging the offending online while “hiding behind the screens of their mobile telephones and computers”.

One of the “non-contact” alleged offenders, Daniel Rayner, 42, of Whitstable, Kent, repeatedly sought imagery of the abuse of the victim, the court heard. He told the husband: “Passed out ones are obviously better,” and: “Hope she’s being used tonight.”

The husband had shared imagery of other men, Graham Brougham and Sean Peers, assaulting his wife, and when Rayner asked: “So I guessing she had no idea he had stripped and fucked her,” the husband confirmed she did not.

Rayner then asked him: “What did you give her … I’ve always used GHB.”

Rayner denies conspiracy to rape, conspiracy to assault by penetration and conspiracy to administer a substance with intent.

Setting out a timeline of the allegations, the prosecutor Louise Oakley KC told the court that by the start of January 2025: “[The husband] doesn’t appear to have had any new year’s resolutions to stop his offending on his wife.”

“If anything, his resolve to satisfy his own perversity continued,” she said. “With arrangements being made for more and more individuals to attend.”

Two of the defendants, Philip Wild, 59, and Mohammed Sabir, 28, both from Stockport, had sexually abused the woman one after the other in a single night in early 2025, the court heard.

Oakley said Wild was “fully aware” that the woman was not conscious and not able to consent to any sexual activity. “He’d been there before so that was no surprise, and certainly was no deterrent the state he’d seen her in before,” she told the court.

Wild has pleaded not guilty to sexual assault, attempted rape, assault by penetration and conspiracy to rape.

Oakley told the court that Sabir also “knew before he went into the bedroom” that the victim was not conscious, and “at no point” did he attempt to communicate with her, or seek her consent.

The husband later asked him to write a review on a swingers website, the court heard, and the jury was shown the review, which read: “Really easy to set up a meet. It was my first [experience] fucking someone’s wife in front of them, so thanks for that. Hope to meet again soon.” Sabir denies rape and conspiracy to rape.

In May 2025 the husband made contact with another stranger on the site, the court heard. Jordan Wallace, 31, a building supplies employee from Wythenshawe in Manchester, said he was “pissed” and asked the husband to pick him up.

He wrote: “13 mins if [you] wanna pick me up [and] me [and] [you] will get perverted with your wife.” When the husband declined to collect him, he said he would ride his bicycle over to the address.

Wallace was “fully aware” the wife was unconscious, and “because of her state of consciousness she was not able to consent to any sexual activity”, Oakley told the court.

She said the husband “recalls that at one point Jordan Wallace ‘got rough’ with [his wife] and he had to actually push him away, saying, ‘What the fuck are you doing?’ telling him he was going to wake her up”.

Wallace later apologised but asked the husband to send the videos he had taken, which he did, the court heard. Wallace has pleaded not guilty to rape and attempted rape.

In June 2025, the husband subjected his wife “to a humiliating, degrading and sustained sexual assault” over the course of three hours, and again invited two men in succession into their home to assault her.

The second man, Alan Keelan, 42, an industrial cleaning firm worker also from Wythenshawe, had climbed on top of the unconscious woman wearing a pair of black women’s knickers and attempted to have sex with her, the court heard.

When he was unable to, he sexually assaulted her before climbing off the bed, the court heard. Keelan denies attempted rape and assault by penetration.

In July last year, the husband again posted on the swingers website, and made contact with the sheet-metal worker Richard Townsend, 38, the court heard. Townsend was aware the woman was not conscious when he proceeded to sexually assault her, assisted by her husband, the jury was told.

The woman’s leg moved, and “it was clear she was restless and stirring, so together they decided enough was enough for that night and he left”, the court heard. Townsend, of Heywood, Greater Manchester, denies assault by penetration.

Jonathan Kirk, 43, from Stockport, was described as “more cautious” than the other defendants, and first requested to meet the husband at his own house.

The husband visited Kirk at his house in August and showed him photographs of the woman passed out and being abused by him. He told Kirk he would consider drugging his wife, and Kirk, a paramedic, told him that “could be very dangerous”, the court heard.

Later, Kirk took a taxi to the husband’s house. “As a senior paramedic, he cannot have failed to notice she was not conscious. He is trained to spot that,” Oakley told the court. “The truth is, he knew and that’s how he wanted her to be.”

After having sexual intercourse with the woman, “Jonathan Kirk stood up and said, ‘fucking hell, wow’ and how great it had been before he left”, the court heard.

Two other men are also on trial. Robert Stewart, 70, of Stockport, denies rape, conspiracy to rape and conspiracy to administer a substance with intent.

David Graves, 60, of Ilkeston, Derbyshire, denies “non-contact” offences of conspiracy to rape, conspiracy to assault by penetration and conspiracy to administer a substance with intent.

Peers, 38, from Stockport; Brougham, 74, from Cheshire, and another man, Keith Fotheringham, 59, from Dundee, have pleaded guilty to offences against the same woman.

Karl Lindsay, 56, from Somerset has also pleaded guilty to “non-contact” offences. All will be sentenced alongside the husband, at a later date.

#177. Kosovo: Outrage as authorities accused of not helping femicide victim

Despite reporting her husband to the police and pleading for protection, Arlinda Zekaj was brutally murdered. Now, thousands in Kosovo are protesting what they say is a dramatic failure of state institutions.

Arlinda Zekaj repeatedly asked the Kosovo police for protection. They said she was exaggerating.

On October 25, the 49-year-old once again reported her husband to the police. This time, he was arrested. But Zekaj also asked the police for additional protection — this time against her husband's family. But to no avail.

The police drove Arlinda Zekaj home. Her killer was waiting. At 8:58 p.m., Zekaj got out of the squad car. A short time later, the police received an emergency call. When officers arrived at Zekaj's house at 9:10 p.m., they found only her body.

The 49-year-old had been brutally murdered in her bedroom with an axe. By that time, the alleged perpetrator — her brother-in-law, Albert Sylaj — had fled.

The murder, which took place in the village of Vitomirica, near the city of Peja in western Kosovo, has sparked numerous public protests and demonstrations involving thousands of people across the country.

It was the eighth femicide in Kosovo in 2026 — and apparently the most brutal.

More so than almost any other femicide in the country in recent years, Zekaj's death demonstrated that one of the things that made her killing possible was the fact that police and prosecutors ignored the victim's pleas for help .

One of the first protests took place on October 26, when a group of feminists emptied buckets of red paint on steps outside the seat of Kosovo's government in the capital, Pristina.

"The state killed Arlinda" read a banner that the activists hung at the entrance to the building.

In the days that followed and on November 1, public anger brought thousands of people to streets across Kosovo. Many carried photographs of murdered women. One slogan appeared again and again: "The government has blood on its hands."

The protesters are demanding that charges be brought against the police officers and prosecutors responsible for handling Zekaj's case.

They are also calling for the resignation of Interior Minister Xhelal Svecla and Justice Minister Donika Gervalla, whom Kosovo Prime Minister Albin Kurti appointed national coordinator for protection against domestic violence in March.

Both Svecla and Gervalla have rejected calls to step down.

Kurti acknowledged that certain state institutions had "failed."

In many ways, this was a murder foretold.

In the summer, Zekaj reported her husband to the police for threatening her. A court in Peja subsequently issued a protection order, granted her temporary custody of their son and gave her the right to remain in the family home.

The protection order allowed her husband to continue living in the same house, provided that he did not come within 2 meters (about 6.5 feet) of her. Outside the home, he was required to keep a distance of at least 20 meters.

Zekaj had decided against moving to a shelter because she wanted to continue living in her own home with her son.

After the protection order was issued, she repeatedly made reports to the police, including about threats from her brother-in-law Albert Sylaj.

Three days before the killing, on October 22, Zekaj reported that members of her husband's family, including Sylaj, were in her home against her wishes. According to her account, he threatened her with the words: "Leave or I'll kill you."

Sylaj was questioned only as a witness in the case against Zekaj's husband.

Police arrested him near Peja on October 27. He is now under investigation for murder.

Since 2010, 55 women in Kosovo have been killed by partners or family members — often after years of documented abuse and ignored reports to law enforcement.

In recent years, successive Kosovo governments have adopted a number of laws and regulations aimed at better combating violence against women.

These measures include the possibility of life sentences for perpetrators in femicide cases.

Activists say the authorities responsible for enforcing these measures — including police, social services, prosecutors and courts — have failed to implement them consistently and in a coordinated manner.

In an article for the newsportal Nacionale, the lawyer Njomeza Zejnullahu wrote that "after every murder, we hear the same word: failure. 'The institutions have failed.' I disagree. Failure is when something happens once, when a system that normally works breaks down at one point. When the cases keep repeating themselves, when the victims are almost always women and each of them had previously knocked on the door of an institution, it is no longer a failure. It is a collapse of the state and society in relation to women in general."

In the aftermath of Zekaj's killing, several senior police officers from Peja and the surrounding area have been suspended. A prosecutor who had handled Zekaj's complaints has also been suspended.

For the protesters in Kosovo, however, this is not nearly enough.

"All those involved within the chain of responsibility must not only be removed from office but also criminally prosecuted for failing to fulfill their official duties," Liridona Sijarina, a political scientist and feminist activist with Kosovo's Kolektivi Feminist (Feminist Collective), told DW.

"That includes not only police officers, prosecutors and judges, but also mayor of Peja Gazmend Muhaxheri and, ultimately, government ministers," she said.

Sijarina said feminist activists were also calling on the Interior Ministry to create a dedicated unit that would treat women's safety as a matter of national security and focus specifically on preventing femicides.

She said the government should make its draft legislation on affordable social housing a top political priority and ensure that women who have experienced domestic violence are eligible for social housing.

"In our view, the behavior of the institutions so far has deepened women's distrust of the state and, as a result, allowed violence against them to escalate," Sijarina said. "The government now has the opportunity to show whether it truly takes women's lives seriously by clearly responding to our demands."

This article was originally published in German.

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

#173. UK government considers smart glasses ban over secret filming concerns

Minister hints at restriction on selling technology ‘to certain users’ amid worries over surreptitious filming of women

The government is considering placing restrictions on smart glasses amid concerns the technology is being used to surreptitiously film women, a minister has said.

Speaking in the House of Commons, the safeguarding minister, Satvir Kaur, hinted at a ban on selling the technology “to certain users”.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

#171. The 2011 discovery of neo-Nazi killers aired biases in German policing

Many questions still surround the National Socialist Underground whose existence was revealed by neo-Nazi terrorist Beate Zschäpe on November 4, 2011. How has German society reacted to NSU crimes? Has justice been done?

Halit Yozgat was killed at point-blank range on April 6, 2006, in Kassel, in the German state of Hesse. Before him, eight other men with immigrant backgrounds had also been shot point-blank since September 2000. The 10th and final person killed by the group Germany would come to know as the National Socialist Underground did not fit this pattern: She was a woman. Police officer Michèle Kiesewetter was killed by the NSU on April 25, 2007, in Heilbronn, in the German state of Baden-Württemberg.

Four and a half years after Kiesewetter's death, the identities of the people behind the murders came to light. On November 4, 2011, Beate Zschäpe, who is now imprisoned with little likelihood of release before 2035, revealed the existence of the NSU. The group also included Uwe Böhnhardt and Uwe Mundlos, who were found dead in a burned-out RV on the same day.

The two men are believed to have died in a murder-suicided following a failed bank robbery in the eastern German city of Eisenach, under circumstances that have never been fully clarified. In a macabre confession video that Zschäpe sent to the media, they boasted that they had shot and killed nine men on racist grounds.

One of the many unanswered questions is why Kiesewetter was also killed. No definitive answers were provided either in the first NSU trial before a Munich court, which lasted from 2013 to 2018, or in numerous parliamentary investigative committees.

Fifteen years after the neo-Nazi terrorist group was exposed, the families of the murder victims are increasingly losing hope that any new information will come to light. One such opportunity was the trial of Susann Eminger — who was sentenced in July by a court in Dresden, in the state of Saxony, to two years of probation for supporting the terrorist group.

Eminger helped Zschäpe assume a different identity during doctor's visits and when dealing with the police by letting Zschäpe use her health insurance card and ID card. However, the court was unable to prove that she was aware of the series of murders committed by the NSU.

Relatives of the murder victims and the survivors of a nail-bombing carried out by the NSU in the western city of Cologne say the verdict beggars belief. "Ten people were executed in cold blood, and countless others were injured in bombings," said Michalina Boulgarides, whose father, Theodoros, was shot dead in Munich on June 15, 2005.

"When will the victims get justice?" Boulgarides asked in response to the suspended sentence handed down to Eminger in the second — and likely final — NSU trial. Eminger's husband, André, had received a two-and-a-half-year prison sentence in 2018 in the first criminal trial against the terrorist group, but he was required to serve only two-thirds of it. That, too, has outraged the victims' families.

Neo-Nazi Ralf Wohlleben completed his 10-year prison sentence for aiding and abetting murder in the first NSU trial in May. This means that Beate Zschäpe remains the only person convicted in connection to the NSU who is still incarcerated; she is likely to remain behind bars until 2035. All other investigations by the Federal Prosecutor's Office against suspected NSU supporters have been dropped.

Though legal proceedings appear to be largely concluded, the broader societal debate surrounding the unprecedented series of murders and attacks carried out by the nationalist terrorists continues. Two key projects, however, are making slow progress: the national memorial center planned for Nuremberg (Bavaria) and the memorial on Keupstraße in Cologne.

Nuremberg is an obvious choice for a national documentation center for the victims of racism and right-wing extremism. It was here where the NSU murdered its first victim, Enver Şimşek, a florist, followed by two others. Another man was badly injured here in what is believed to be the first NSU bombing.

What's more, Nuremberg also played a major role during the Nazi era. Adolf Hitler held Nazi Party rallies featuring mass marches and speeches.

The German Bundestag has been considering the idea of an NSU Documentation Center since 2024. However, plans for a swift implementation fell by the wayside because the governing coalition at the time, led by Chancellor Olaf Scholz (SPD), collapsed prematurely. The project is now set to be completed by 2030.

A response from the federal government to a parliamentary inquiry from the Greens provides initial details: Just under €9 million ($10 million) has been earmarked to create the central memorial site under the leadership of the Federal Agency for Civic Education (BpB).

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In Chemnitz, in the eastern German state of Saxony, a documentation center on the NSU complex has been in operation since 2025, the year in which the city was European Capital of Culture. The title of the exhibition, "Open Trial," speaks to its broad, ambitious approach as a space for education, research and events.

Gamze Kubaşık gave a speech at the opening. Her father, Mehmet, was shot and killed by the NSU in Dortmund on April 4, 2006. In fact, Chemnitz was where the terrorists Zschäpe, Mundlos and Böhnhardt had been in hiding for years. "This is also where they planned my father's murder," said Gamze Kubaşık, highlighting the significance of the location. "The support they received from the local neo-Nazi scene enabled them to carry out their schemes and reinforced their ideology."

The executive directors of the Chemnitz-based organization, Nora Krzywinski and Esther Gerstenberg, believe a national memorial and documentation center dedicated to the NSU complex is necessary. "However, it cannot replace the work being done at the actual sites of terror and to address the social conditions that gave rise to it," they emphasized in their response to a written inquiry from DW.

The guiding concept for the exhibition, Krzywinski and Gerstenberg said, is to highlight the perspectives of those affected and to link remembrance with an examination of the persistence of racism and the responsibility of the state and society. "That is why we do not view our work as a substitute for a national memorial site but rather as part of an integrated system that connects sustained local, regional and national efforts to remember and come to terms with the past in a lasting way."

Though efforts to address the multifaceted NSU complex in Chemnitz have already taken concrete form, the residents of Keupstrasse in Cologne are still waiting for the promised memorial to the 22 people injured in the June 9, 2004, nail-bomb attack. According to media reports, however, construction cannot begin until 2030 at the earliest, because old buildings around the planned site must first be demolished and new ones erected.

This means that it will take more than a quarter century until a memorial honoring the victims of the NSU bombing on Keupstrasse can be erected where it took place. Now, 15 years after the NSU was revealed, the German Bundestag has agreed to hold a debate on Thursday.

It is titled: "Commemorating the Victims, Living Up to Our Responsibilities and Taking Today's Threats Seriously."

This text was originally written in German.

#169. UK and Caribbean to begin ‘dialogue’ within next year after slavery reparations call

Antigua and Barbuda’s prime minister says there was a consensus at Commonwealth summit on way forward

The UK has agreed to “structured dialogue” in the next year with Caribbean nations affected by the slave trade and its legacy, Commonwealth leaders have said.

Gaston Browne, Antigua and Barbuda’s prime minister, said there was a “consensus” in discussions between governments about a way forward on slavery reparations at the Commonwealth heads of government meeting (Chogm).

“There’s camaraderie and where we have issues that potentially could have divided us, we’re able to come to a consensus on the way forward,” he said.

Browne also praised UK prime minister Andy Burnham for being “very assertive in addressing [the] issue”.

“Perhaps there are some who believe that he may have dodged the issue as a relatively new prime minister. I think he has shown a significant amount of courage and goodwill, and I can tell you that he has developed a lot of goodwill with the Caribbean nations.

“And this structured dialogue that he has proposed, we are hoping that it will take place sometime within the next 12 months. We have also agreed in principle that by the end of this year, or early next year, that the whole framework for the dialogue would be established.”

Browne said the region would not be making “unreasonable demands on the United Kingdom”.

Burnham announced on Friday that he wanted to begin a structured dialogue and was open to Britain apologising, but ruled out reparations.

Instead, he proposed discussions on debt resets for smaller Commonwealth nations and announced £40m ($54m) for critical infrastructure in the Caribbean over the next three years.

A 10-point plan drawn up by the Caribbean Community’s reparations commission, which outlines the region’s vision for reparations, specifically asks for “debt cancellation” – not debt restructuring – and “monetary compensation”, arguing that former colonies’ “existing debts are intergenerational and are ultimately and intimately tied to centuries of colonial extraction”.

Asked whether Caribbean nations would urge Burnham to approach the talks with an open mind on reparations, Browne said: “We see the prime minister as an open-minded person, and the issue certainly will be raised.

“On the commencement of the structured dialogue, he has taken his position and we are very clear on our position that reparations are important, that reparations must be part of this structured dialogue and we remain very optimistic that we will come to a consensus on the issue.”

Leading figures in the Caribbean reparatory justice movement have said that debt relief and development assistance from the UK cannot be a replacement for reparations for centuries of slavery.

Ralph Gonsalves, a former prime minister of St Vincent and the Grenadines and a senior figure in the reparatory justice movement’s Repair Campaign, said: “When I listened to Andy Burnham, and I listened to the king, and they’re talking about what is helpful is not reparations, but for them to work on specific things like debt relief, and education and health, and climate change infrastructure.

“Now, all those things are within the 10-point plan. But they are also within the sustainable development goals of 2015, which we all signed on to at the United Nations,” he told the Guardian.

He added: “There are things in the reparatory justice 10-point plan which deal with issues … the first one is the ecology, and then psychological repair. And for people like members of the Rastafari faith, or brothers and sisters who may wish repatriation, that’s something we have to work out with African countries, those who want to go back. But there are financial implications in that. So you’ll have to have a separate carve-out on the basis of the 10-point plan.”

Labour MP Bell Ribeiro-Addy, chair of the House of Commons’ all-party parliamentary group for Afrikan Reparations, said the question was fundamentally about “agency”.

Speaking at an event on Tuesday to launch a “global reparations caravan”, which will travel across countries to educate and engage people in the reparations movement, she said: “So when it comes now to reparative justice, and as we move forward in the movement, I have to be clear with everybody, it has to be about agency, not making deals here and there, because agency was the crime. When you think about what was taken from people of African descent when they were enslaved, it was their agency, their right to decide all of these different things about their lives.

“So when reparative justice arrangements are being made, it absolutely has to be made with those who were affected, because if it is done to us, it is not reparations. I don’t know what it is, but it is not reparative justice.”

Gonsalves said the danger of reducing reparatory justice to development assistance was that such assistance remained discretionary on the UK’s part. “We have to understand the specificities of reparatory justice as distinct from general sustainable development goals. They’re shaping the discussion only within the frame of sustainable development goals,” he said.

Prof Kingsley Abbott, a human rights lawyer and director of the Institute of Commonwealth Studies at the University of London, also stressed the importance of harmed communities having a decisive say in what reparations should entail.

Speaking at the global reparations caravan launch on Tuesday, he said: “It’s for you to say. It’s your story. It’s your lived experience. But it’s time to start listening to those who continue to suffer from those crimes even today. It’s time to listen to you and to listen and learn from you.

“I reflected on my work as a human rights lawyer in different parts of the world. And in that time, I’ve met with many different victims of serious human rights violations, atrocity crimes, and their families and their descendants in places like Guatemala, Colombia, Nepal, Myanmar. And here in Antigua tonight, I’m meeting with all of you.

“Wherever those victims of those terrible crimes and their families were from, and whatever their faith, whatever their ethnicity, whatever their beliefs, they all had one thing in common. When I met with them, they all said that they wanted justice. And they wanted not only justice, but they wanted to be made whole again.”

A spokesperson for the Foreign Office said: “The prime minister wants to build stronger relationships to renew the relevance of the Commonwealth in a new era. We will do that by facing our past together and focusing on a future where every person living within it has equality, security and hope.

“We are already committed to working with Commonwealth partners on issues facing the world, including the role of private finance in tackling global debt issues, addressing the climate crisis, and supporting economic development. We will continue to do so.”

#166. Milburn review to call for jobs ’revolution’ for young people with disabilities

Exclusive: Government should introduce ‘flexi-jobs’ alongside subsidies and skills support, former minister will say

The British state should create and subsidise paid jobs for thousands of disabled young people as part of an “opportunity revolution” aimed at reversing years of labour market discrimination, the Milburn review of youth employment will recommend.

The review, due to report this month, will say the government should introduce “flexi-jobs” alongside employment subsidies and intensive skills support to make it easier for disabled and chronically ill 16- to 24-year-olds to get work.

It will argue that the state should step in to underwrite jobs for young people who are locked out of employment and training opportunities, as part of an overhaul of what it sees as an outdated welfare and skills system.

About a million young people are now classified as “Neet”, meaning they are not in education, employment or training. Nearly half (45%) of those are disabled or in ill health. Disabled young people are three times as likely to be Neet as their non-disabled peers.

The review’s author, the former Labour cabinet minister Alan Milburn, has described soaring numbers of Neets as a moral and economic crisis, warning that failure to tackle rising youth disengagement from work risks creating a “lost generation”.

He has rejected what he has called “myths” about young people being “snowflakes”, insisting that the crisis in youth mental health is real, most youngsters want to work, and it is the duty of government and employers to provide support and opportunities.

The review will say young people with disabilities need a flexible and supportive approach to work that acknowledges fluctuating medical conditions and lack of work experience, and recognises the importance of part-time roles in helping them build confidence.

One eye-catching recommendation is for the UK to adopt a Danish-style “flexi-job” scheme. This guarantees disabled participants employment while allowing them to set working hours – in some cases starting with as few as four hours a week – that suit their individual capacities, with wages topped up by the government.

It will also call for creative ways of shaping jobs to individual capabilities, such as “job carving”: splitting the three component parts of a single entry-level role and matching youngsters to each of those roles. A retail role that comprises stock-filling, till work and locking up would become three job opportunities.

Nearly half of 24-year-old Neets have never worked, and Milburn argued in his interim report earlier this year that abandoning a cohort of Covid-era youngsters coping with high levels of depression, anxiety, autism and ADHD would have a scarring effect on their life chances and lifetime earnings.

Although the government has said it will not endorse “crude cuts” to benefits, Milburn has suggested that the state should switch some of the £4bn a year it spends on disability benefits for young people into comprehensive, high-quality employment and training support.

However, he will probably face pushback from campaigners, and potentially backbench Labour MPs, who argue that job support should not be funded by disability benefit cuts that would push many young people into poverty and further away from the job market.

Milburn’s proposals are in part a recognition that young people with disabilities face huge barriers around recruitment processes and inflexible job design, and many businesses are unlikely to create job opportunities for them without government support.

The plans have echoes of the postwar Remploy scheme, which created state-funded workplaces providing “sheltered” employment for disabled ex-service personnel. At its height, Remploy had 90 factories across the UK, the last closing in 2013.

The review will urge large employers to voluntarily adopt employment targets for disabled 16- to 24-year-olds, although it is expected to stop short of recommending statutory quotas.

As well as creating jobs, the review will call for a boost for job support, including supported internships, placements, specialist job matching and coaching, and support for employers to provide workplace adjustments.

Milburn who has been critical of the limited ambitions of existing government job support programmes, has previously called for a “turbo-charging” of specialist internships for young people with special educational needs who are moving on from school and college.

The most common conditions reported by disabled Neets were depression, bad nerves or anxiety (23.8%), followed by autism (22.7%) and other health problems or disabilities (18.5%), according to the latest official figures.

→ Baca artikel asli

#165. French blood banks targeted by refrigerator cyberattack

A cyberattack had attempted to destroy blood plasma by raising the temperatures in cold storage facilities past the boiling point.

Blood banks in France were included among cyberattacks that hit refrigeration systems in several European countries on Sunday and Monday, French authorities said on Wednesday.

The French Blood Establishment (EFS) said that a "cybersecurity incident" targeted a refrigeration service provider affecting control systems at three cold storage sites for plasma and blood samples.

The cyberattack attempted to raise temperatures to extremely high levels but it was warded off before any damage could be done. The plasma in the affected refrigerators was transferred or secured, the EFS said.

"No health consequences have been identified, and no blood products were lost," the EFS said in a statement, adding that its distribution operations were not interrupted.

The cyberattack was first reported on Wednesday by the French weekly newspaper Le Canard Enchaine, which said the temperatures had been programmed to reach a boiling 110 C (230 F), rather than -30 Celsius (minus 22 Fahrenheit).

"Monitoring and alarm systems quickly detected the anomalies, allowing staff to take action to ensure that no blood products or samples were compromised," the EFS said.

France's cybersecurity agency and Health Ministry said an investigation is underway, but did not indicate who could have been responsible.

The Health Ministry said Wednesday that remote access to the affected refrigerators was disabled, and credited monitoring and alarm systems with ensuring the safety of blood supplies.

Coolworld, the company supplying the refrigeration equipment, said the vulnerability was related to a component from a "third-party supplier and was outside Coolworld's direct control."

On Sunday and Monday, a cyberattack targeted refrigeration systems at supermarkets in several European countries, including France and Germany, destroying large quantities of frozen food.

It was reminiscent of a similar attack three weeks ago targeting refrigeration systems at dozens of French supermarkets.

The attacks have cost supermarkets millions of euros in wasted food.

On Wednesday, Dutch supermarket chain Ahold Delhaize said it expects the cyberattack in October to reduce quarterly sales by as much as €250 million ($281 million).

"While most of the disruption was contained within two weeks, work continues to fully restore our normal operations," CEO Franz Muller said.

#164. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#160. Fact check: Examining viral narratives after the US midterm elections

As Democrats celebrate major gains, false claims about election fraud have spread. Viral posts alleging illegal voting and election rigging are intended to fuel doubt about the integrity of the vote. DW checks the facts.

Democrats swept to victory in a closely watched US midterm election, delivering a strong rebuke to President Donald Trump and his Republican party.

At the same time, social media was awash with false claims that the election was fraudulent, including doctored screengrabs from media outlets, invented stories of illegal ballots and accusations of vote tampering.

Even as the votes are still being counted in some races, DW Fact check took a closer look at some election day disinformation.

Claim: "A highly coordinated scheme to steal an election is happening in real time," a right-wing media outlet claimed in a X post, viewed more than 700,000 times.

Attached was a security camera video showing a man helping voters at a polling booth in Lewiston, Maine, flipping through papers, marking ballots and speaking to them as they wrote. Other users in the thread claim they are Somali-American voters.

Although the footage itself appears genuine, the claim that it shows a coordinated effort to steal the election is not supported by the available evidence.

Maine's election law says: "A voter who is unable to read or mark the ballot because of physical disability, illiteracy or religious faith may request another person ... to assist the voter in reading the ballot or marking the ballot according to the voter's wishes."

That hasn't stopped the right-wing account, The Maine Wire,  the outlet that shared the video, from calling the practice a "blatant violation of state law."

The same account had been posting similar videos in the run-up to the elections. The outlet stated it had requested the footage from Lewiston City Hall under the state's own Freedom of Access Act. Authorities in Lewiston told DW Fact Check that it had denied a request for surveillance footage because ballots were visible in the recording and disclosure could violate Maine election law.

The Maine Wire reporters were accused of harassingSomali voters and recording people without permission.

The videos have been shared on Facebookand Reddit and a Trump-appointed federal attorney for Maine said his office would review evidence concerning complaints of voter intimidation and election interference.

The secretary of state's office told the local news outlet Maine Morning Star that it hadn't received any credible reports of interpreters committing election fraud.

The Department of Justice deployed election monitors to Lewiston and other jurisdictions ahead of the midterms as part of what it described as the largest election-monitoring effort in its history.

Claim: "Over twenty thousand fraudulent ballots uncovered in FBI raid of Houston storage locker warehouse all for Democrats" was the claim in a viral post on X that was viewed more than 400,000 times.

The Federal Bureau of Investigation confirmed that no such operation took place in a written statement to DW: "That information is false and no election-related operation occurred," it said.

The post's images also showed that the story was made up.

The first image, of an FBI truck at a crime scene, was taken from a different news story. A reverse image search reveals that the photo was taken during an FBI raid of the Fulton County elections warehouse in Georgia earlier this year, during which ballots from the 2020 election were confiscated. The truck has "FBI Atlanta" written on it.

The second image shows a storage facility in Houston. It was taken from the self-storage company Public Storage's website. There were no indications that an FBI raid took place there.

And the third image is an image of ballots with the words "city and county of San Francisco Department of Elections" printed on them, showing that there is no connection to an alleged raid in Texas.

The account that posted the story belongs to a man who calls himself a "Digital Content Creator for Pro Trump Administration TrumpNews.baby"

Claim: "I cannot accept that the result tonight was free or fair. It is simply not possible for somebody with the radical ideology of James Talarico to win an election in Texas fairly" was the wording attributed to Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton in an X post designed to look like a still from the broadcaster NBC, which was viewed more than 700,000 times.

Paxton did not say this and this is not an authentic image of an NBC broadcast. In fact, the opposite is true: The Republican candidate for Senate conceded the race to Talarico.

"Let me start off by congratulating James Talarico. I will commit that I will be praying for him that he will do good for Texas and this nation," Paxton said to supporters at a campaign party.

Talarico won in a stunning upset, becoming the first Democrat to win a seat in the US Senate for Texas in over 30 years.

Republicans have been more likely to cast doubts on election results since Trump's failed attempt to challenge the outcome of the 2020 election. But Trump himself said he would acknowledge Tuesday's results.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"I am absolutely willing the accept those results," Trump told the New York Times, in the days leading up to the midterms.

That wasn't the only social media post that used a doctored NBC graphic to spread falsehoods. Another viral X post allegedly shows the news outlets NBC’s 2026 election results with graphics and images claiming to show that Republicans won.

Though it is a convincing fake at first glance, the graphics don't match NBC's normal background colors or shading.

Other users shared fake vote totals, too. This post, viewed 1.7 million times, shows doctored results with a Republican victory in Congress and the Senate.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Alima de Graaf contributed to this report.
