#96. Tears, celebrations and selfies: US midterms in pictures

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Hugs, cheers and smiles broke out as the Democrats picked up a string of victories in Republican strongholds, delivering them the Senate and the House.

The win gives them control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Meanwhile, it was a challenging night for Republican supporters, after a succession of losses in reliably Republican states.

Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms defeated Republican Rick Jackson, a billionaire businessman endorsed by President Trump.

Bottoms becomes the first black woman elected to be governor of Georgia.

The state has not had a Democratic governor in more than 20 years.

Republican Senator Jon Husted shed a tear whilst surrounded by his family after losing his Ohio seat to Democrat Sherrod Brown.

Another Democrat victory was seen in Georgia where Senator Jon Ossoff was re-elected, giving him a second term.

Speculation surrounds Ossoff, considered a potential leading contender to run for the Democrats in the next presidential election in 2028.

James Talarico, the new US senator in Texas, and Gina Hinojosa, the state's new governor, toasted their successes.

In Portland, Maine, supporters reacted to the news that Democratic Senate candidate Troy Jackson won his seat. He beat Republican veteran Susan Collins who has been in the Senate for nearly 30 years.

Susan Collins was pictured looking at election results coming in on a laptop.

The mood was different for Republican supporters at a midterm election night watch party in Des Moines, Iowa. Republican Ashley Hinson lost her Senate seat to Democrat Josh Turek.

Democrat Chris Pappas made history as the first openly gay man elected to the Senate, beating his rival Republican Senator John Sununu.

Pappas celebrated his New Hampshire win on stage with his husband.

Attendees paused for thought after a watch party for Maine Republican Senate candidate Susan Collins, following her defeat by Democrat Troy Jackson.

In North Carolina, the Democrats secured the night's first flip of a Senate seat.

The state's former Democratic governor, Roy Cooper, won against Republican candidate Michael Whatley.

A supporter raised a jubilant fist to celebrate the North Carolina win.

In Michigan, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed will become the first Muslim US senator. He told voters: "You took a chance on the unlikeliest of candidates."

The mood was subdued amongst Republican supporters, after a string of defeats in reliably Republican strongholds.

#94. Telstra apologises after customers mystified by phantom missed call notifications

Telco blames its MessageBank service and apologises for customer confusion

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Thousands of Telstra users have reported phantom missed-call notifications on their mobile devices with some messages arriving days after the actual phone calls.

The company acknowledged an issue with its MessageBank service on Wednesday but said the issue had been fixed. Telstra was investigating the cause, which was linked to a technical issue.

The company said the error was not the result of an outside influence or hack.

“We identified an issue with our MessageBank service yesterday, which was fixed last night,” a Telstra spokesperson said in a statement.

“Some customers may have noticed several delayed missed call and voicemail notifications arriving at once, some dating back to several days.

“We’re sorry for the confusion this may have caused. There was no impact to triple zero calls, voice calls or mobile data.”

Telstra’s Facebook page received more than 3,000 comments from confused users.

“I got a missed call on my work mobile this afternoon from my personal mobile which was locked in my cupholder while driving and listening to an audio book via bluetooth,” LisaMaree Collier wrote.

“No call on the call log of the private phone. So confused as I’ve not called myself in months (last time I misplace the phone).”

Another customer, Steven Alexandrou, said: “My dad was right next to me and my phone said he called me. I was wondering what was going on.”

A Telstra user named Simone Lou said the explanation seemed to be “why my boss called me after hours thinking I’d called him when I hadn’t 😑”.

Phil Richards also lamented the news: “Damn, I thought I was just really popular all of a sudden.”

Telstra has 26m mobile services on its network.

The company had a major nationwide outage in July that affected millions of customers, took some train services offline and prevented shops from using Eftpos machines. It blamed a “software defect” at the time, with CEO Vicki Brady saying Telstra would take the steps needed to prevent such an outage from happening again.

#93. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#89. Anthony Zurcher: Democrats push deep into Trump heartland on historic night

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: The key moments from the US midterm elections

Democrats have torn up Donald Trump's political map on a night that will send shockwaves through American politics for years to come.

With a string of major Senate victories in states that have been reliably Republican for decades - and in Kansas's case, nearly a century - a blue tidal wave crashed across the nation, sweeping Republicans out of power.

Halfway through President Donald Trump's second term, the Democrats now have decisive control of the House of Representatives and have wrested a majority back in the Senate.

They will be able to frustrate Trump's agenda, and launch investigations into his administration. A historic third impeachment attempt could also be on the cards.

Few imagined such an outcome was possible in the months after Trump returned to the White House with unified Republican control of Congress in January 2025.

As the public mood turned against him and his party, Democrats had hopes of retaking the House.

But they contemplated a Senate map that appeared unfriendly, populated by states Trump had won comfortably just two years ago.

Instead, Democrats rewrote the political equations with victories in Texas, Maine, Kansas, Ohio, Iowa and North and South Carolina.

In comments to the BBC's US partner CBS, Trump pledged to strike deals with congressional Democrats, adding that he didn't want to have to spend his final two years in office rejecting their legislation.

He somewhat undermined that sentiment later, posting a picture to Truth Social of his trademark red baseball hat with the word "veto" emblazoned on it.

The president went on to note that his name wasn't on the ballot this year, but if it had been Republicans would have won a decisive victory.

Trump had helped elevate many of those Republican candidates, however, and had urged voters to pretend he was up for re-election.

REACTION: Trump accepts Democrats did a 'good job' in election

THE BIG MOMENTS: Ecstatic scenes that greeted key results

In the Senate, Democrats flipped at least six seats, comfortably securing a majority.

Adam Hamilton won in Kansas - a state that last elected a Democrat to the Senate almost 100 years ago during the Great Depression.

James Talarico beat Republican Ken Paxton in Texas' Senate race, ending a 38-year Democratic losing streak in statewide elections there.

In South Carolina, Annie Andrews beat Darline Graham, who had been appointed to the seat after the death of her brother, Senator Lindsey Graham. Few analysts had that seat in play even a few months ago.

Five-term Republican Susan Collins conceded Maine's Senate election to Democrat Troy Jackson.

Paralympian Josh Turek flipped the Iowa Senate seat held by retiring Republican Senator Joni Ernst. Democrats also wrested from Republicans a Senate seat in Ohio, with Sherrod Brown's comeback victory over Jon Husted.

Meanwhile, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed has beaten Republican Mike Rogers in Michigan's bitterly fought Senate race.

Nor was that the end of the electoral carnage for Republicans.

Ruby-red Texas witnessed another seismic result in the governor's race.

Democratic challenger Gina Hinojosa ousted the incumbent Republican Greg Abbott, who was earlier this year seen as the prohibitive favourite.

Democrats also took governors' races in Iowa, Ohio and Georgia, which made history by electing the first black woman to such a post.

Meanwhile, the party has won a House majority for the first time since 2022.

They are projected to win 245 House seats and the Republicans 166 seats.

Democrats celebrate in Maine where the party won a crucial Senate race

A look at the key races offers plenty of reasons why.

Democrats flipped House seats in Texas, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, Iowa, Kentucky and Colorado, among others. And they were poised to make gains in Virginia and Indiana.

Black Democrats in the southern states turned out in record number, helping to carry their party to victory. And Hispanic voters, whose support had helped secure Trump's presidential victory in 2024, swung decisively back to the Democrats.

Democrats won all three House races in the overwhelmingly Hispanic congressional districts along the Texas-Mexico border.

And Hispanic voters helped Democrats hold House seats in Florida that the state's Republicans had sought to flip by redrawing the congressional district lines.

In fact, the congressional redistricting battles that had dominated headlines for months last year ended up being a bust for the Republicans.

In Texas, they had hoped their line-drawing would net five new seats for their party. But on Tuesday they may end up losing one seat.

If there is a silver lining for conservatives, Florida as a whole still appears to tilt to the right.

Republican Senator Ashley Moody narrowly won re-election, as did congressman Byron Donalds, who was elected the Sunshine State's first black governor.

And embattled Republicans were able to hold governorships in Vermont and New Hampshire. But the bright spots were few and far between.

Just two years ago, Trump won the presidency with 48% of the vote. He claimed an electoral mandate.

He then went about governing like it, pushing sometimes unpopular policies on trade and immigration, and launching an Iran war that is opposed by most of the American public.

On Tuesday, voters gave him the kind of stinging rebuke that will not be easily forgotten.

#88. David Jones goes into administration, with store closures expected and thousands of jobs at risk

Future of Australia’s upmarket department store in jeopardy after years of steep losses, and outer suburban and regional locations vulnerable

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia’s premier upmarket department store David Jones has collapsed into voluntary administration, putting thousands of jobs at risk amid warnings “difficult decisions” lie ahead to salvage the 188-year-old retailer.

The upheaval follows years of steep losses and underperforming stores that left the heritage retailer falling behind on rent, culminating in a breakdown of negotiations with its primary landlord, Westfield owner Scentre Group.

Staff were informed on Thursday that the David Jones board had appointed administrators from McGrathNicol, before the retailer’s main financier promptly installed FTI Consulting as receivers.

Under the dual appointment, FTI will take day-to-day control of operations, while administrators McGrathNicol seek a buyer or fresh injection of capital.

Christopher Hill, of FTI Consulting, said the process allowed for “an opportunity to restructure the business”.

“We will manage this process carefully, mindful of the impact on some stakeholders but also recognising the need for difficult decisions,” Hill said.

The process will almost certainly trigger immediate store closures, though receivers will try keep the core network open.

Analysts expect closures to hit outer suburban and regional mall locations hardest, likely sparing premier CBD flagship stores.

David Jones employs more than 6,000 staff across a network of 38 stores.

The David Jones chief executive, Erica Berchtold, said there was a “well developed plan on how to reset the business” and that the retailer would work with the administrators, receivers and its secured lender.

Creditors are scheduled to meet next week, according to the Shop, Distributive and Allied Employees’ Association.

The national secretary of the SDA, Gerard Dwyer, told the ABC the top priority was to retain as many jobs as possible.

“We do not want to see the end of David Jones, so perhaps it is a smaller model that then rebuilds and goes from there,” Dwyer said.

“Smaller is better than no David Jones, and a smaller David Jones can then deal with some of the challenges that they’ve obviously been facing.”

The administration could leave unsecured creditors with substantial losses, with unpaid product suppliers, some of which had already begun redirecting stock to their own retail outlets, and shoppers holding unredeemed gift vouchers most at risk.

Angus Kidman, from consumer comparison site Finder, said customers with David Jones gift cards should use them as soon as possible.

“If the business doesn’t find a buyer, that gift card will almost certainly be worthless,” Kidman said.

“Stores appear to be accepting gift cards for now, so don’t delay. That also applies to any discounts you’ve earned through David Jones Rewards.”

The collapse marks the first time David Jones has been in administration, having previously weathered nearly two centuries while cycling through almost every type of ownership model, from public listing to foreign conglomerate ownership and, most recently, private equity.

Anchorage Capital Partners, which bought David Jones from South Africa’s Woolworths in 2023, has now lost operational control after failing to turn the struggling retailer around.

It could re-bid for the business through the administration process or a rival buyer, such as billionaire businessman Solomon Lew, who controls Myer, may get involved.

The retailer’s main landlord, Scentre, said on Wednesday that David Jones had not paid rent since September.

“We have made a generous offer to Anchorage Capital that would have facilitated the continuation of David Jones trading and that offer has disappointingly been rejected by them,” Scentre said in a statement.

#86. Everything you need to know from the US midterm elections

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

Democrats have won both the US Senate and House of Representatives, taking control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Projected results have provided the party with a number of things to celebrate - and rival Republicans some reasons to commiserate.

Here is everything you need to know about some of the big Democratic wins and some historic firsts.

Democrats have tallied up victories in states that were comfortably won by Trump two years ago in the presidential election.

With James Talarico winning a seat in the US Senate seat and Gina Hinojosa being elected state governor, Democrats scored two statewide victories in Texas - ending a string of defeats they have endured for almost two generations.

James Talarico, the new US senator in Texas, and Gina Hinojosa, the state's new governor, toast their successes

Talarico's supporters went beserk as the projected results arrived at an election-night party, wrote the BBC's Gary O'Donoghue. Attendees hugged, kissed and drank to a reality they dared not believe would come true.

Hinojosa became the first Latina to be elected governor of the state.

Among the other states where history was made by the Democrats were Kansas, where Adam Hamilton took a closely-fought Senate seat - giving the state its first Democratic senator for nearly a century.

LIVE UPDATES: All the latest developments

BOWEN: Trump's loss limits powers at home, but it will likely be different abroad

RESULTS: How America voted in maps and charts

EXPLAINER: Five reasons why Trump did so badly

ZURCHER: Midterm carnage weakens Trump but he still wields power for now

REACTION: Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms 'disaster'

NEWSLETTER: Sign up for Anthony Zurcher's analysis in your inbox

Senior party figures who cheered the results included Hakeem Jeffries, the top Democrat in the House of Representatives, who is now poised to take over as the Speaker of the lower chamber of Congress.

"Today begins the great American comeback," he told a crowd of supporters.

If Jeffries' colleagues choose to keep him as the leader of their party's delegation in House, he will be the next Speaker, taking over from Republican Mike Johnson and becoming the first black person to fill that role.

Trump spoke throughout the evening to a number of US media outlets, striking a conciliatory tone and conceding that the Democrats did a "good job".

He said he would accept the results and that he was looking forward to working with the opposition party - albeit he would still have the power, as president, to veto legislation that Democrats advance through Congress.

Trump distanced himself from some of the Republican candidates who contested the election, saying: "In some cases they didn't like the candidates, and in some cases those candidates were not my friend."

He acknowledged that the US-Israel war on Iran, which has impacted consumer prices in the US, had affected the vote. Trump argued that high fuel prices meant voters could not see how well his economy was doing "as much as they should have", adding: "The gasoline was caused by Iran."

Listen to Americast: Democrats make historic shift in US politics

Abdul El-Sayed has become the first Muslim person elected to the US Senate - after claiming victory in Michigan in what was one of a handful of closely fought contests. His win means the seat stays in Democratic hands.

He defeated Republican opponent Mike Rogers, who received the surprise backing of El-Sayed's Democratic rival in the primary contest, Haley Stevens, who changed her endorsement at the eleventh hour.

The race was one of the bitterest of the midterms. Trump invoked 9/11 and even called El-Sayed a "jihadist" without citing any evidence, prompting a backlash and accusations of Islamophobia.

El-Sayed triumphed in one of the bitterest contests of the midterms

El-Sayed's supporters went wild when he took the stage to celebrate his claimed victory, wrote the BBC's Nada Tawfik. He said: "Up until right about now, you might have noticed I always say 'inshallah,' or 'God willing,' whenever the outcome of this race was mentioned."

He went on to say: "But today, tonight, we can turn that 'inshallah' into 'alhamdulillah'. For the right-wing ops, that just means 'praise be to God.'"

The defeat of Susan Collins, who has served as the Republican senator for Maine for three decades, was one indicator of the scale of the Republican losses.

She was felled by Democratic candidate Troy Jackson, a logger who sought to point to his background to persuade blue-collar Mainers to vote for him.

In a statement reported by US media, Collins conceded defeat and said it had been the "honour of my life" to serve the state for 30 years.

The BBC's Helena Humphrey described a sombre atmosphere at Collins's watch party in the city of Maine - where the song Closing Time was played.

There was a sombre atmosphere at the watch party for Republican Susan Collins

Collins entered the race as one of the most vulnerable incumbent Republicans seeking re-election, and found that her tactic to distance herself from Trump did not work.

Ashley Hinson suffered the same fate in Iowa, losing that closely-fought Senate seat to Josh Turek. Hinson had slightly distanced herself from the president, urging him to end the war with Iran and breaking with him on easing beef tariffs, while at the same time defending Trump's broader tariff policies as important.

Among the other firsts of the night were in Florida, where Republican Byron Donalds has become the state's first black governor, following a hearty endorsement from Trump.

Donalds, who will take over the job from Ron DeSantis, said his state was the "envy of the nation", and that he would work to make the state more affordable.

The result was one of the few high points of an otherwise difficult evening for the Republicans.

A win for Bryon Donalds in Florida was one of the few high points for Republicans

In the neighbouring state of Georgia, Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms, the former mayor of Atlanta, won the governor's race, becoming the first black governor and first woman to be elected to the state's highest office.

The BBC's Carl Nasman described ecstatic scenes as the race was projected blue. Bottoms defeated Republican Rick Jackson, a billionaire businessman and healthcare executive. The state has also not had a Democratic governor for more than 20 years.

Elsewhere, Democrat Chris Pappas became the first openly gay man elected to the Senate. He celebrated on stage alongside his husband in New Hampshire.

Pappas defeated former Republican Senator John Sununu in the race, whom he thanked on Tuesday, saying: "We all owe John Sununu a big debt of gratitude for his public service for the people of New Hampshire."

Watch: "I am ready to fight" - Senator-elect Chris Pappas celebrates New Hampshire win

In Colorado, Melat Kiros became the first woman elected to Congress from Gen Z - meaning those who were born between 1997 and 2012.

#84. Beau Lamarre-Condon tells double murder trial he suffered ‘daily bullying’ when working for NSW police

Lamarre-Condon gives evidence in court after pleading not guilty to murdering Jesse Baird and Luke Davies in early 2024

Live updates: Beau Lamarre-Condon gives evidence in murder trial

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Former officer Beau Lamarre-Condon has alleged he heard homophobic abuse and was bullied when working for New South Wales police, as the 31-year-old gave evidence in his double murder trial.

Lamarre-Condon is accused of fatally shooting couple Jesse Baird and Luke Davies with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home on 19 February 2024.

He has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

Lamarre-Condon on Thursday started giving evidence at the NSW supreme court in front of a 15-person jury. He was the first witness for the defence.

Wearing a light grey suit, a dark coloured tie and a white collared shirt, Lamarre-Condon vowed to tell the truth.

He accused a former supervisor of making homophobic remarks while he was working as an openly gay police officer.

“He would say things such as ‘I used to bash gay people while in Jindabyne’,” Lamarre-Condon told the court on Thursday.

On one occasion, when responding to a domestic violence report, the supervisor allegedly said “Let’s go get these fucking faggots”, the jury was told.

Lamarre-Condon told the jury on another occassion, a coworker shot himself in the head. He recalled in court the last conversation the pair had.

“I said ‘How are you’ and he told me he was good. Nothing appeared out of normal to me, nothing about his behaviour suggested what he was about to do,” Lamarre-Condon told the court.

“[He later] went to his desk, took out his gun whilst off duty and shot himself in the head at work.”

Lamarre-Condon’s coworkers found their deceased colleague, the court was told.

The accused responded to questions about confronting situations he had encountered while working as a police officer. He told the court he could remember 12 to 15 incidents graphically.

Lamarre-Condon said he had “seen people that have jumped in front of trains, I have seen multiple hangings”.

“Daily bullying” led Lamarre-Condon to transfer to the police youth command division to “escape” officers “tormenting” him at Surry Hills police station, he told the jury.

Asked about his relationship with Baird, Lamarre-Condon told the jury: “For the months of October and November 2022 it was friends with a sexual relationship. When we would meet up it was of the sexual nature. By December 2022, that’s when we began going on romantic dates.”

Prosecution lawyers wrapped up their case earlier on Thursday morning. They called more than 50 witnesses, played hours of CCTV footage and shared pages of social media messages over the first seven weeks of the trial.

Lamarre-Condon’s defence team has previously claimed Baird and Davies were accidentally shot in two separate struggles with the accused when he was acting in “self-defence”.

The officer cleaned up the scene and disposed of the bodies afterwards because he “panicked considerably”, the defence lawyer John Stratton SC previously said.

The trial before Justice Natalie Adams continues.

#80. How America voted in maps and charts

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

It's been an extraordinary night for the Democrats in the US midterms.

They have taken control of the Senate and the House of Representatives too.

The results in the Senate are beyond what the Democrats would have expected. North Carolina was a likely pick-up but the previously deep red state of South Carolina was a huge shock and Kansas had not returned a Democratic senator since 1932.

Texas, Iowa, Ohio and Maine also turned blue and the Democrats held Michigan. There are two seats remaining, Alaska and Mississippi, both are on a knife edge.

The shift from Republican to Democrat happened across the country. Some of the biggest swings were in Texas where the Democrats took the Senate seat and the governor.

The BBC's partner CBS News projected the Democrats would win the House just after 03:00 GMT on Wednesday (22:00 EST Tuesday).

Now it's a question of how big that win will be as counting continues.

Two significant seats are Florida 14 and Florida 25 which the Democrats have held. This is a blow to the Republicans' attempt to redraw the boundaries in their favour.

The redistricting saw both districts move from having a majority of 2024 Kamala Harris voters to having a majority of people who voted for Donald Trump.

Florida 22, which was also redrawn to favour the Republicans, saw a victory for the Democrats too.

A huge poll of voters suggests women turned out in numbers and most voted Democrat. Men on the other hand were evenly split between the two main parties.

Hispanic voters look to be one of the key groups in the elections in Florida. The poll suggests this group backed the Democratic Senate candidate Angie Nixon by 55% to 44% for the Republican Ashley Moody.

This is a turnaround from the estimated 55% of Hispanic voters who backed Donald Trump in 2024.

The poll also asked what issues voters were most concerned about. It suggests that across the country the economy was the biggest issue, followed by the state of democracy.

When voters were asked if Donald Trump's policies impacted economic conditions, the poll suggests that two-thirds believe they had made things worse.

#77. ‘An insult to his culture’: Italian chef sacked for not putting pineapple on pizza loses bid to get job back

Australia’s Fair Work Commission heard that Davide Tamburella was sacked after refusing to use the controversial topping

With its sea foam-green and white decor, exposed brick columns and “classic Italian dishes” served atop European oak tables, La Costa was billed as an elegant slice of Italy in the urban heart of Queensland, Australia’s sunshine state.

“We promise, it’ll only take a moment to realize you’re not in Brisbane anymore, you’ve made it to the Italian Riviera,” the now closed restaurant’s website still reads.

But for its ​​disgruntled former head chef, it seems, just one moment – or, more specifically, one ingredient – was all it took to break the spell conjuring the “luxurious splendor of Italy’s coastline” and to plonk his diners right back into the nightclub district of Queensland’s Fortitude Valley.

That ingredient was pineapple. On a pizza.

A Fair Work Commission ruling filed on Tuesday heard that Davide Tamburella was sacked from La Costa on 20 May, a fortnight after he was hauled before his boss, Elliot Ingram, “to address performance concerns”.

Among those concerns was the Italian chef’s refusal to put pineapple on pizza – on the grounds that to do so was “an insult to his culture”.

In the Australian state, in which the spiky tropical fruit is so revered that a giant (or at least big) monument has been built in its honour, it is this refusal that many would consider a sacrilege.

But for both Tamburella and Ingram, this would prove a costly refusal.

The Italian was more than three years into a 186 visa process – an employer-sponsored pathway to permanent residency. Ingram says he waited as long as he legally could to notify authorities that he was no longer hiring Tamburella in the hope that the Italian’s visa application would be approved before the restaurateur was legally obliged to withdraw his sponsorship. It did not, meaning the temporary migrant would have to start the 186 process again.

For Ingram, there was the cost of doing business.

“We had a large function come in, and they were spending in excess of $15,000, and the guy paying for the whole thing says: ‘Look, I need to get some ham and pineapple pizza,’” Ingram said.

The Fair Work Commission heard that Tamburella acknowledged that this was the third time he had refused this direction, instead requesting Ingram “check how important it is for Italians not to put pineapple on a pizza”.

And while Ingram said the divisive tropical pizza was not his own first choice, 17 years in hospitality and more than a decade of running restaurants had taught him to “pick your battles”.

“Do I go and buy a ham and pineapple pizza? It’s probably not my absolute favourite,” he said, tactfully. “But I’m not going to turn my nose up at it, that’s for sure. And, look, we have had ham and pineapple pizza on the kid’s menu previously.”

Tamburella alleged that he was terminated in contravention of the Fair Work Act, with his dismissal partly motivated by him suffering a workplace injury after he struck a metal handrail, fracturing his right hand in two places.

The FWC commissioner, Jennifer Hunt, dismissed Tamburella’s general protections application on the grounds that it was filed too late and did not meet the criteria for exceptional circumstances.

La Costa closed in July when its lease expired, with Ingram citing the high cost of doing business in Fortitude Valley.

Tamburella was contacted for comment. He is now employed at another Italian restaurant. Pineapple does not appear on its menu.

→ Baca artikel asli

#76. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#66. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#63. Beau Lamarre-Condon trial live updates: accused double murderer gives evidence in high-profile case

The 31-year-old former police officer is accused of fatally shooting couple Jesse Baird and Luke Davies in Sydney in 2024

Former police officer Beau Lamarre-Condon enters witness box in Sydney double murder trial

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Lamarre-Condon tells the jury his experience at work made him feel suicidal:

double quotation markAll the constant pushback, the bullying, torment, being told I can go back to picking up dead bodies … [it] made me feel suicidal. I didn’t want to be at work any more.

His voice begins shaking as he describes how he felt:

double quotation markAs a youth engagement officer, I have a personal relationship with these young offenders.

Me not showing up to work is not like showing up to a general duties [shift] … This is me letting down young people that needed me as a role model, needed me to help them, needed me to be there for them.

It was too much for me and it made me feel like having thoughts to take my life.

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he tried to take his own life, picking up tissues and wiping his face as he gives his evidence.

Lamarre-Condon afraid he would lose job over medication

The officer told the court he wanted to start taking medication that belonged to his stepfather, who was prescribed medication for depression and anxiety:

double quotation markI was very fearful that I would lose my job as a police officer if I was prescribed any medication.

Lamarre-Condon said one of his superiors said that if he “finds out anyone is on medication, that we would not be able to keep our job”.

He told the court he made a complaint about this.

Lamarre-Condon tried to avoid Paddington after breakup with Baird

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he tried not to go to Paddington after their breakup in November 2023:

double quotation markI did not want to see Jesse, did not want to run into Jesse.

Lamarre-Condon tells court he could have obtained a gun from youth gang member

Lamarre-Condon says his work in the youth command meant he could have gotten his own gun:

double quotation markI work with youth gangs. Some of their families are in the Comanchero … I could easily go to these kids that look up to me ‘here’s four grand … go get me a gun’, and have a gun six hours later.

Shortly after Baird asked Lamarre-Condon to refrain from contacting him in November 2023, Lamarre-Condon says he saw scratches on the rims of his Tesla.

double quotation markI parked so far away from the curb, I believed it was Jesse who did this.

He says his mother had seen Baird’s car earlier that day.

double quotation markI know tit-for-tat isn’t right, I did go to the street where Jesse parked his car and did pour water into the fuel tank.

Lamarre-Condon has told the court he posted a bouquet of flowers on to his story around mid-November 2023 while Baird was in Melbourne.

He says it was to give the impression it was from Baird and wanted to see if another man Baird was seeing would message him and say, “well I’m also dating Jesse”.

Asked if he had gone through Baird’s phone around this time, Lamarre-Condon tells the court he looked at Baird’s messages with that other man while the TV presenter was in the shower.

Baird later called Lamarre-Condon and asked if he went through his phone to block someone:

double quotation markI denied, denied, denied … within 10 hours of denying it I essentially came clean.

He and Baird went back and forth about the flower messages, he tells the court:

double quotation markI told Jesse the flowers were sent to my mum by her partner … that wasn’t true.

I then sent a voice not by text message, saying yes I did block [the man] on your phone, that was to try to insinuate they were from you when they weren’t.

I completely owned up to it, I was truthful and honest 10 hours later.

Lamarre-Condon tells the jury his experience at work made him feel suicidal:

double quotation markAll the constant pushback, the bullying, torment, being told I can go back to picking up dead bodies … [it] made me feel suicidal. I didn’t want to be at work any more.

His voice begins shaking as he describes how he felt:

double quotation markAs a youth engagement officer, I have a personal relationship with these young offenders.

Me not showing up to work is not like showing up to a general duties [shift] … This is me letting down young people that needed me as a role model, needed me to help them, needed me to be there for them.

It was too much for me and it made me feel like having thoughts to take my life.

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he tried to take his own life, picking up tissues and wiping his face as he gives his evidence.

Crisis support services can be reached 24 hours a day: Lifeline 13 11 14; Suicide Call Back Service 1300 659 467; Kids Helpline 1800 55 1800; MensLine Australia 1300 78 99 78; Beyond Blue 1300 22 4636

Lamarre-Condon says he was having problems at work in 2023 after getting pushback on a proposal to run equestrian therapy for women.

When he escalated the issue beyond his bosses, Lamarre-Condon says direct supervisors were not happy:

double quotation markThey pretty much told me, if I wanted to involve them I can go back to picking up dead bodies on the street and not [go] back to youth command.

Lamarre-Condon tells court Baird was ‘screaming’ and thought he was a ‘random’

Lamarre-Condon says Baird did not realise it was him and began to gave chase.

double quotation markHe was screaming out to who he believed was a random saying, ‘Let’s go cunt you’re lucky I don’t have a fucking bat or a knife with me.’

When Lamarre-Condon realised he still had Baird’s wallet and phone, he threw it back at Baird.

The wallet fell on the footpath and the phone went down the drain, he tells the court.

Lamarre-Condon tells court about a night Baird left key out for him

Lamarre-Condon says he and Baird had a conversation about staying the night at the Paddington home on 17 August 2023 and he agreed to leaving the key in the letterbox.

He tells the court he arrived after 10pm, opened the back door and left a bobby pin in the front door.

The jury heard Baird was asleep when Lamarre-Condon walked into his room.

Baird’s phone was on his bedside table charging.

Lamarre-Condon says he picked up the phone and put it in airplane mode so it would not vibrate.

The officer then dropped the charger and “panicked”:

double quotation markI have a split-second decision: do I sit here and have a conversation about why I was on his phone … or do I run?

The officer said CCTV footage found on his devices of Baird and another man at the Royal hotel in Paddington was recorded during his police duties.

He coincidentally saw Baird that day and took the video of the screen, before sending him the footage.

Lamarre-Condon told the court he said something like: “I came here to see you, but detective Beau is on the case.”

Lamarre-Condon says sexual relationship with Baird recommenced after birthday dinner

Lamarre-Condon has told the court he saw Baird on his birthday night in 2023 at the Unicorn hotel in Paddington:

double quotation markI did see Jesse, it was a very normal and amicable conversation … We had a normal conversation about normal things.

He said he received a birthday message from Baird earlier that day on social media.

Defence lawyer John Stratton SC asks: “Was there an occasion where you went to Jesse’s house and saw him through the bedroom window?”

Lamarre-Condon told the court he asked Baird if he could collect his things from his home.

He said some time after 21 April, he went to Baird’s house and saw his bedroom light was on through his sheer curtain:

double quotation markI saw Jesse having sex with a man I did not know.

I paused for about 5 seconds … I immediately left, I didn’t stay like a little creep, I didn’t take any photos.

I left … and actually laughed about it with friends … we were no longer together …

Lamarre-Condon told the court this happened on only one occasion.

Lamarre-Condon tells court Baird told him he ‘wasn’t ready for a relationship with anyone’

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he met with Baird on 17 April 2023 and told him he had read the messages between Baird and the other man.

double quotation markI told him this made me feel a bit upset because we were moving towards – I guess being a couple.

Jesse was honest with me [and] became emotional and started crying, said he wasn’t ready for a relationship with anyone not just me.

#59. Nadal set to join Real Madrid board of directors

Rafael Nadal has made a number of appearances on the pitch at Real Madrid matches

Tennis great Rafael Nadal is set to join the board of Spanish football giants Real Madrid.

Nadal won 22 Grand Slam titles, two Olympic gold medals and helped Spain claim five Davis Cups before retiring in 2024.

Real's members will be asked to approve the 40-year-old's appointment to the board at a meeting later this month.

In a statement announcing the move, external, the club said Nadal was "one of the greatest legends in the history of sport and one of the most admired and recognised Spanish athletes in the world".

"It is a great source of pride that a figure of his sporting and human stature, a benchmark of Spanish and world sport, will become part of our board of directors and contribute, from this position of responsibility, to the present and future of our club," Real added.

The tennis legend - a boyhood Real fan who holds member's number 67,778 - included a photo of himself as a child in a club kit in his response to the news.

"I want to thank president Florentino Perez and the board for proposing that I join the board of directors of Real Madrid," Nadal said in his post on X, external.

"I have accepted this proposal with great excitement, a desire to learn, and a willingness to contribute everything I can."

It is currently unclear as to what responsibilities Nadal would hold on the club's board.

Perez was re-elected to serve a new four-year term as Real president in June, having held the office since 2009.

He swiftly appointed Jose Mourinho to a second spell as head coach, hoping to improve the club's fortunes after they went a second season without a trophy in La Liga, the Champions League or the Copa del Rey.

Real are currently third in the league after 11 games - three points behind defending champions Barcelona.

#56. The US-China trade war seems to have subsided – but the sense of calm is misleading

A truce has cooled the trade conflict, but neither country has done anything to limit the outbreak of new hostilities

Donald Trump’s trade wars have largely fallen off the headlines. Fears of new tariffs flare up from time to time when the US president goes off the rails – for instance, threatening to end US trade with countries with which it has a deficit unless the Federal Reserve cuts interest rates. But the sense that the world’s two largest economies are plowing ahead into an overt economic conflagration has taken a back seat to other, more salient fears, about Trump ending American democracy, AI killing us all and stuff like that.

The sense of calm is misleading. Under the radar, the world is marching toward an economic war of potentially epic proportions, one that, history suggests, could well spill over into the real thing.

The truce reached by Washington and Beijing last year in their escalating trade conflict, which has been extended until next January, lowered the heat between the two – putting an end to the escalation of tit-for-tat tariffs and opening a door to some as yet undefined future trade deal.

But neither China nor the US has done anything to establish the mutual assurances that could limit the outbreak of new hostilities. Rather, they are both focused on gaining the upper hand in what seems like an inevitable next round, in a standoff that both sides have defined as a national security priority, essential to ensure economic, technological and military supremacy.

The new arms race is different from those in the past. Rather than building weapons to blow each other up, China and the US are focusing on ensuring the resilience of the international supply chains on which they rely and figuring out how to constrain – and if push comes to shove, throttle – the supply chains essential to the economic and technological progress of their rival.

The paired strategies go back at least to the Covid pandemic, when China’s Xi Jinping called on the nation to “tighten international production chains’ dependence on China, forming a powerful countermeasure and deterrent capability against foreigners who would artificially cut off supply [to China]”.

Early in the Biden administration, national security adviser Jake Sullivan articulated the American goals: to limit China’s access to a narrow set of advanced technologies with military applications without upending the global economic order. He described the strategy as building a “small yard” of forbidden tech surrounded by a “high fence” of trade and investment controls.

By now, the conflict has breached the bounds of these initial rationales. Restrictions on American tech exports, investments and technology transfers to China have proliferated over the last half decade. And they are hitting not only China’s military capabilities but slowing its technological progress writ large. And Trump’s wanton deployment of tariffs is not helping.

China, on the other hand, is becoming increasingly assertive, leveraging its overwhelming dominance over the supply of critical minerals that are essential to hi-tech industries for civilian and military uses. Last year, it stunned the Trump administration by throttling exports of rare earths and magnets made from them to retaliate against US tariffs and efforts to restrict China’s access to high-end semiconductors and the lithography machines needed to make them.

It has relaxed some of these controls, but it maintains many other restrictions. While they have largely been imposed to retaliate against American actions, they have also been used to hit other countries for a variety of policies it dislikes. The stakes are high. The International Energy Agency has estimated that if China were to block the US’s and Europe’s access to rare earth magnets, it could cost their economies about $1.5tn per year.

Where does the supply chain war go from here? The Trump administration is pouring hundreds of millions into projects to mine and process rare earths and other critical minerals, hoping to close China’s lead. It is also doubling down on efforts to limit China’s access to frontier semiconductors and to bar it from buying the machinery needed to make them.

China, on the other hand, has announced a range of hi-tech export restrictions, covering things such as artificial intelligence chips and solar power technology. Moreover, it has threatened to punish countries that work to reduce their dependence on its mineral inputs.

One hopes there is a path to a peaceful entente. This would probably require some shared understanding of what constitutes a legitimate national security risk that may warrant actions to block supply chains. Defining the “yard” is essential. Frustrations of standard commerce should not be dealt with on a wartime footing.

China will probably dominate renewable energy technologies, especially the solar power industry, into the foreseeable future. It may also dominate the electric vehicle industry. This might hurt in Brussels and Washington, but it’s not worth a war. China, on the other hand, will have to accept that its mercantilistic economic strategy is not tenable for the rest of the world. Its combination of massive export growth with zero import growth is not sustainable.

This might seem doable. And yet the odds that the world economy will remain at peace seem slim. One might recall the Axis powers’ efforts to seize territories as the League of Nations curtailed their access to raw materials in the early stages of the second world war. Or FDR’s blocking of sales of American scrap steel and oil to Japan, which probably contributed to Tokyo’s decision to attack Pearl Harbor. Today, American fighter jets, submarines and missiles use rare earth magnets. What would happen if China blocked access to these materials?

The moment is strange. It’s not that long ago that trade was proposed as perhaps the most powerful tool to deliver world peace, building positive-sum interdependence among nations. That’s over. Interdependence has become a vulnerability. As China and the United States fight over who will control the next generation of technology, trade has become a theater of war.

#53. Iran's chief negotiator says Americans voted 'no to war'

Mohammed Bagher Ghalibaf said Iran "endorsed" the message sent by American voters

Iran's chief negotiator in talks with the US has said that Americans voted "NO TO WAR", after President Donald Trump's Republican Party suffered a stunning defeat in the midterm elections.

"We endorse this message," parliament speaker Mohammed Bagher Ghalibaf wrote on X.

"If the US [government] betrays their public will to serve international paymasters and blackmailers, it will learn our NO comes with a Nightmare," he warned.

Trump acknowledged on Tuesday that the poor results for Republican congressional candidates were partly due to the war and the related surge in petrol prices. But he insisted that he did not regret launching a joint attack with Israel on Iran in February.

In an interview with Fox News, Trump said of the midterm results: "I think a part of it was Iran".

He went on to say: "People agree with what I'm doing on Iran but they really probably weren't willing to do the short term consequences of paying more for gasoline - I understand that."

Speaking separately to the BBC's US news partner, CBS News, Trump said the conflict would end "when I say it ends".

In recent weeks, Trump has warned that the US and Israel could return to all-out war after the midterms if a deal to end the conflict cannot be reached with Iran.

Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei - who has not been seen in public since he was reportedly seriously wounded in the same Israeli air strike on the first day of the war that killed his father and predecessor, Ali - said he did not expect US policy to change.

Khamenei released a statement saying anyone who thought there would be any change from American "hostility towards the Iranian nation" was "gravely mistaken".

He also said the war would have "much wider dimensions" if it resumed.

In a separate development on Wednesday, Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said four oil tankers caught fire after hitting naval sea mines in the Strait of Hormuz.

The IRGC said two of the vessels were still burning, and two additional tankers had been intercepted, according to the semi-official Tasnim news agency. It said they were trying to travel through a prohibited southern route in the Strait of Hormuz.

BBC Verify has been investigating the IRGC claims, and has not found any evidence that four tankers were destroyed.

The UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) centre said a tanker was struck by two unknown projectiles about 14 nautical miles (26km) north-east of Limah, in Oman's Musandam peninsula, on Tuesday evening. No casualties were reported, it added.

On Monday, an Indian crew member of a dhow - a wooden sailing boat - was killed in an attack north-east of Limah. Thirteen other crew members were rescued by Omani authorities.

The IRCG also reported on Wednesday that its forces had shot down another US MQ-9 drone over the Strait of Hormuz.

It made a similar claim on Monday night, when US officials said the US military had carried out limited strikes on Iran's Qeshm Island in response to the IRGC firing on a US aircraft.

US midterms: Democrats push deep into Trump heartland on historic night

In an interview with the Financial Times ahead of the midterms, Trump suggested he was happy with the state of the conflict, and that he did not consider them to be "really in a war".

When asked about what the newspaper described as "strong hints" by Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu that there would be an attack on Iran this week, Trump said they were "locked and loaded if we need to go, but there may be no need for it".

Indirect talks to end the conflict have been stalled for months.

President Masoud Pezeshkian maintained on Tuesday that his government had been trying to resolve the crisis "through dialogue".

"No-one will benefit from this war" if it were to resume, he warned.

He also emphasised that Iran's supreme leader had granted the government powers to negotiate "issues that he had previously considered off-limits".

Iranian foreign ministry spokesperson Esmaeil Baqai said on Monday that the basis of the current indirect negotiations mediated by Qatar remained a seven-step proposal that Iran submitted in September. But he added that the proposal had been "condensed, making it possible to implement it within two days".

The Strait of Hormuz, through which a fifth of global oil and gas shipments usually pass, was effectively closed after the start of the Iran war due to Iranian missile and drone attacks on commercial vessels and the US naval blockade.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the Strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

Iran insisted it would not give up some measure of control over the waterway and began targeting vessels using a southern route through Omani waters that avoids its preferred route through Iranian waters.

To keep exports flowing from Gulf Arab states, the US military has been providing protection for tankers shuttling oil through the Strait of Hormuz.

#51. Trump’s troubles with Iran could open the door to China

Beijing launches diplomatic salvo to woo Gulf leaders and bolster its influence in the region

China has held a flurry of meetings with Gulf leaders in a sign that Beijing is stepping up its diplomatic efforts in the Middle East.

The diplomatic salvo comes before a highly anticipated meeting between Donald Trump and Xi Jinping when the US president is expected to attend the Apec conference in Shenzhen, China, on 18 and 19 November.

Xi held talks with Haitham bin Tariq, Oman’s sultan, in a historic state visit to Beijing on Monday.

President Xi’s foreign minister, Wang Yi, met Saudi Arabia’s national security adviser, Musaad bin Mohammed Al Aiban, and the deputy secretary of Iran’s national security council, Ali Bagheri Kani.

Haitham’s trip to Beijing is the first time that an Omani head of state has visited China since the two countries established diplomatic relations nearly 50 years ago. According to a Chinese readout of the meeting, Xi warned that the Middle East should not become an arena for “great power rivalry and geopolitical conflicts”.

He said: “China has always been actively committed to stopping conflict and promoting peace, and hopes to contribute wisdom and solutions to resolving the regional security dilemma.”

Wang said after his separate talks that China was willing to “play a constructive role in helping the region emerge from the shadow of the pandemic and achieve lasting peace and stability”. In his meeting with Bagheri Kani, Wang said China supported Iran “in safeguarding its sovereignty and security”. According to China, the Iranian official said his country looked forward to China “playing a more important role in international and regional peace”.

When the US and Israel launched joint strikes on Iran in February, China was credited with pushing Tehran toward ceasefire talks in Pakistan, bolstering its status as a regional mediator.

Beijing’s influence in the Middle East is “no longer what it used to be”, said Shen Dingli, a Shanghai-based scholar of international relations. But he added: “Given China’s increased investment and trade in the region … its influence cannot be ignored.”

As the war has dragged on, China’s diplomatic efforts have been muted. Its biggest economic leverage over Iran is the fact that it buys about 90% of the Middle Eastern country’s crude oil exports. Between April and June, it slashed its crude imports by 30%, with the total in June plunging to the lowest level in a decade. That saved the global economy from a major spike in inflation. According to some estimates, China’s cut in oil imports shaved $30 or more off barrel prices of Brent crude, a global benchmark.

Beijing has otherwise done little to broker peace in the Middle East. That is partly because it does not see the conflict as being its responsibility to resolve. In March, Wang said the war “should never have happened” and that the US and Israel would find “no popular support” for the strikes.

China is also a beneficiary from the damage that the fighting has done to the US’s global reputation.

According to findings published in July by the Pew Research Center, a Washington-based thinktank, China is now viewed more favourably than the US in a majority of 36 countries surveyed.

The pain caused by the long-term disruption to the strait of Hormuz, the waterway through which much of the world’s oil and gas flows, also poses a risk to China’s economy. Shortages of energy cause inflation, which risks reducing global demand for Chinese goods.

Tehran and Washington are locked in intensive talks to reach an agreement, which could include reopening the strait. Trump has repeatedly threatened to renew military action against Iran after Tuesday’s midterm elections in the US, a move which would cause alarm in Beijing.

Trump’s desperation to reach a deal in the Middle East may also provide an opportunity for China to present itself as a regional peacemaker, bolstering Xi’s status before their meeting. Beijing brokered a detente between Saudi Arabia and Iran in 2023 but since then its diplomatic impact in the region has been limited.

Shen said China’s desire to improve diplomatic relations with Middle Eastern countries as well as the US were “mutually reinforcing” trends.

China’s foreign ministry spokesperson, Mao Ning, said on Monday that China would send special envoy Zhai Jun to Iran, Oman and Saudi Arabia in early to mid-November.
